\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\biggl\lvert\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\biggr\rvert\geq(\epsilon/2)F_{p}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Pr}\left[\biggl\lvert\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\biggr\rvert\geq(\epsilon/2)F_{p}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle~~~~~~+\text{Pr}\left[\biggl\lvert\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\biggr\rvert\geq(\epsilon/2)F_{p}\mid\neg\mathcal{H}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\neg H\right]

\displaystyle\leq(2/3)\delta^{2}\cdot\text{Pr}\left[H\right]+(1-\text{Pr}\left[H\right])

(130)

We have
\text{Pr}\left[H\right]\geq 1-n^{-c}
, for any constant
c\geq 1
. Since,
\delta=n^{-O(1)}
, we can choose
c
so that
\text{Pr}\left[H\right]\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{10}
. Then, Eqn. (
A
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle\leq(2/3)\delta^{2}(1-\delta/10)+\delta/10

\displaystyle\leq\delta\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem 53
(Re-statement of Theorem
16
)

For each
0<\epsilon<1
and
7/8\geq\delta\geq n^{-c}
, for any constant
c
, there is a sketching algorithm that
(\epsilon,\delta)
-approximates
F_{p}
with sketching dimension

O\left(n^{1-2/p}\left(\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta)+\epsilon^{-4/p}\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)\log n\right)\right)
and update time (per stream update)
O((\log^{2}n)(\log(1/\delta)))
.

Proof:
The correctness of the algorithm follows from Lemma
52
.

The algorithm uses
O(C\log(n))=O(n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta)+n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-4/p}\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)\log(n))
sketches at the lowest level structure. The other structures at levels
0,1,2,\ldots,L
are geometrically decreasing in size with common ratio
\alpha
, and hence, the space is dominated by a constant times the space used at level
0
, that is,
O(C\log n)=O(C\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)\epsilon^{-4/p}(\log n))
sketches.

The degree of independence used is
O((\log n)+(\log(1/\delta))
for the roots of unity sketches and the hash functions for the
AvgEst
structure at each level. Consequently, the time taken to process a stream update is
O((\log n)+(\log(1/\delta)))
.

Appendix B
Extending the algorithm for
n^{-\Omega(1)}
failure probability to
2^{n^{-\Omega(1)}}
failure probability

We have so far shown an algorithm for computing an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-approximation to
F_{p}
when the failure probability
\delta\geq n^{-c}
, for some constant
c
. We will now extend the analysis to the case when
\delta
is
n^{-\omega(1)}
, that is,
\delta=o(1/\text{poly}(n))
.

B.1
The
ghss
structure and the event
\mathcal{G}

Consider the event
\mathcal{G}
defined as a conjunction of events earlier in Section
3
.
In
[
Gan15
]
, the set of good events
\mathcal{G}
as defined is a proper superset of the events constituting
\mathcal{G}
in this work, and that set was shown to hold with probability
1-n^{-\Omega(1)}
.
So far, we have looked at the case when
\delta=n^{-O(1)}
. In this case, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{G}
holds with probability
1-n^{-c^{\prime}}
, for any constant
c^{\prime}
. Since, this follows from the previous treatment in
[
Gan15
]
, no separate arguments were given.

Since now we consider the case when
\delta=n^{-\omega(1)}
we have to at least show that
\mathcal{G}
(as defined in Section
3
) holds with probability
1-\delta^{\Omega(1)}
. In order to do so, we first prove an extension of Lemma 34 from
[
Gan15
]
that shows that not only is
C_{L}=n^{\Omega(1)}
( as shown in
[
Gan15
]
), but for a suitable choice of the parameter
\nu=\Omega(1)
,
C_{L}\geq(\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))^{1+c}
, where,
c>0
is a constant. Using a theorem from
[
SSS93
]
, this then implies that
\mathcal{G}
holds with probability
1-\delta^{\Omega(1)}
. We first show that the
smallres
event holds with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
, and consequently,
goodlastlevel
also holds with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
.

The events
nocollision
,
accuest
and
smallhh
are unchanged and hold with probability
1-n^{-\Omega(1)}
. These however are not part of the good event
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 54

[Extension of Lemma 25 in
[
Gan15
]
.]
Let
\alpha=1-(1-2/p)\nu
, for
\nu<(\ln 2)/8
. Then, for
p>2
and
\displaystyle\nu<\frac{p/2+2/p-2}{(1-2/p)^{2}(4/\ln 2)}
, we have, (1)
C_{L}\geq n^{\Omega(1)}
and (2)
C_{L}\geq(\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))^{1+c}
, where,
c>0
is a constant depending on
p
.

Proof:

Let
\alpha=1-(1-2/p)\nu
and let
\gamma=1-\alpha
.
Following the notation of
[
Gan15
]
, we have,
C=Kn^{1-2/p}
, where,
K=\kappa\cdot\epsilon^{-4/p}\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)
. As shown in Lemma 25 in
[
Gan15
]
,

\displaystyle C_{L}\geq K^{1+4\gamma/(\ln 2)}\cdot n^{1-2/p-(2/p)(4\gamma/(\ln 2))}\kern 5.0pt.

(131)

From the lower bound of
\Omega(n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta)
for
(\epsilon,\delta)
-approximating
F_{p}
, we have that

\log(1/\delta)=O(\epsilon^{2}n^{2/p})
or that,
n\geq\Omega\left(\epsilon^{-p}\log^{p/2}(1/\delta)\right)
. Let
G=\log(1/\delta)
. Substituting in Eqn. (
131
), we have,

\displaystyle C_{L}

\displaystyle\geq K^{1+4\gamma/(\ln 2)}\cdot n^{1-2/p-(2/p)(4\gamma/(\ln 2))}

\displaystyle\geq(\epsilon^{-2}G)^{(2/p)(1+4\gamma/(\ln 2))}(\epsilon^{-2}G)^{(p/2)\left(1-2/p-(2/p)(4\gamma/\ln 2)\right)}

\displaystyle=(\epsilon^{-2}G)^{(2/p)+(p/2)-1-(1-2/p)(4\gamma/(\ln 2))}\kern 5.0pt.

(132)

The exponent of
\epsilon^{-2}G
in (
B.1
) equals

\displaystyle(p/2)+(2/p)-1-(1-2/p)(4\gamma/\ln 2)=(p/2)+(2/p)-1-(1-2/p)^{2}(4\nu/\ln 2)

(133)

using the fact that
\gamma=1-\alpha=(1-2/p)\nu
.

In Eqn. (
133
), note that
p/2+2/p=(\sqrt{(p/2)}-\sqrt{2/p})^{2}+2>2
, since
p>2
. By choosing
\displaystyle\nu<\frac{p/2+2/p-2}{(1-2/p)^{2}(4/\ln 2)}
, the exponent of
\epsilon^{-2}G
in Eqn. (
B.1
) is greater than 1.
Therefore,
\nu
can be chosen small enough so that

C_{L}\geq(\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))^{1+c}

for some constant
c>0
.

Lemma
54
ensures that for levels
l\in[0,\ldots,L]
,
C_{l}>\omega(\log(1/\delta))
. This ensures that
\log(1/\delta)\leq\lceil C_{l}e^{-1/3}\rceil
. This in turn implies that Lemma 26 of
[
Gan15
]
holds with
d=O(\log(1/\delta))
provided, the hash functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}
are drawn from a
d
-wise independent family. It then follows that the events,
\textsc{goodtopk}(\{B_{l}\}_{l\in L}),\textsc{small-u}(\{C_{l}\}_{l\in L})
, each holds with probability
1-(\delta/n)^{\Omega(1)}
. This implies that
smallres
holds with probability
1-(\delta/n^{\Omega(1)})
.

The top-most level
L
of
ghss
uses the deterministic compressive sensing based algorithm
[
CRT06
,
Don06
]
for the recovery of
x_{iL}
for those items
i
that hash to level
L
. These techniques guarantee the deterministic recovery of any
k
-sparse
n
-dimensional vector
y
such that
\left\lVert{y-x}\right\rVert_{2}\leq C^{\prime}\min_{k\text{-sparse }x^{\prime}}\left\lVert{x^{\prime}-x_{L}}\right\rVert_{1}
, where,
C^{\prime}>1
is a constant, using
m=O(k\log(n/k))
measurements. Let
x_{L}
denote the vector of frequencies of items that are sampled into level
L
, that is,
(x_{L})_{i}=x_{i}
, if
i\in\mathcal{S}_{l}
and
(x_{L})_{i}=0
otherwise. Following the arguments of Lemma 28 in
[
Gan15
]
, we have with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
that,
\bigl\lvert\{i\in\mathcal{S}_{L}\}\bigr\rvert\leq 2C_{L}
. Therefore,
x_{L}
has at most
O(C_{L})
non-zero entries, and by compressive sensing, using
m=O(C_{L}\log(n/C_{L}))=O(C_{L}\log n)
measurements, these entries are exactly recovered. Hence, with this modification,
goodlastlevel
holds with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
.

We now prove Lemma
17
.

B.1.1
Number of items not satisfying
goodest
or
nocollision

In this section, we try to calculate the number of items
i
in the
ghss
levels that do not satisfy
goodest
(i)
, with probability at most
1-O(\delta)
. These are the items that can possibly be un-estimated, or underestimated, or overestimated, i.e., may cause
\hat{F}_{p}
to be in error.

For the analysis, consider the
CountSketch
(C,s)
structure
[
CCFC04
]
with
s
hash tables, denoted
T_{1},\ldots,T_{s}
, each consisting of
C^{\prime}=O(C)
buckets. The hash functions for the respective tables are denoted by
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}
. Let
\{\xi_{ri}\}_{i\in[n]}
denote the family of Rademacher variables used for the sketches in table
T_{r}
. For the initial part of the analysis, we just consider one independent copy of the
s
repetitions, and denote the hash table by
T
, the hash function by
h
and the Rademacher family
\{\xi_{j}\mid j\in[n]\}
. For each
b\in[C]
,

T[b]=\sum_{j:h(j)=b}x_{j}\xi_{j}\kern 5.0pt.

Fix
i\in[n]
. Following the definition of
estimate
for
x_{i}
obtained from the single table copy under hash function
h
as given in
[
CCFC04
]
, we have,

\hat{x}_{i}=T[h(i)]\cdot\xi_{i}=x_{i}+\biggl(\sum_{j\neq i}x_{j}\xi_{j}\chi_{ij}\biggr)\xi_{i}

(134)

where,
\chi_{ij}
is an indicator variable such that
\chi_{ij}=1
iff
h(i)=h(j)
. We will say that the
estimate for
i
is good under
h
if the following event holds:

\text{Good Estimate}(i):~~\bigl\lvert\hat{x_{i}}-x_{i}\bigr\rvert<\frac{\sqrt{24}}{\sqrt{C}}\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}

We consider the following question: Let
S_{k}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subset[n]
be any given fixed subset of
k
distinct items, where,
k=C/20
. Obtain an upper bound for the probability

\text{Pr}\left[\geq\text{Good Estimate}(i_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge\neg\text{Good Estimate}(i_{k})\right]

Let
x^{h(i)}
denote the
n
-dimensional vector
\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\cdot\chi_{i,1},x_{2}\cdot\chi_{i,2},\ldots,x_{n}\cdot\chi_{i,n}\end{bmatrix}
. Clearly,

\left\lVert{x^{h(i)}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{j\in[n],j\neq i}x_{j}^{2}\chi_{i,j}

Taking expectations,

\mathbf{E}\left[\left\lVert{x^{h(i)}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]=x_{i}^{2}+\frac{\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-x_{i}^{2}}{C}\leq x_{i}^{2}+\frac{\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{C}\kern 5.0pt.

By Markov’s inequality applied to the non-negative variable
\left\lVert{x^{h(i)}}\right\rVert^{2}-x_{i}^{2}
, we have,

\text{Pr}\left[\left\lVert{x^{h(i)}}\right\rVert^{2}-x_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{8\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{C}\right]\geq\frac{7}{8}

Define the event
GoodBucketNorm
(i)
as follows:

\emph{GoodBucketNorm}(i):\left\lVert{x^{h(i)}}\right\rVert^{2}-x_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{8\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{C}

Fix the hash function
h
. This fixes the function
\chi_{ij}
. From Eqn. (
134
), we have,

\bigl\lvert\hat{x}_{i}-x_{i}\bigr\rvert=\biggl\lvert\sum_{j\neq i}x_{j}\xi_{j}\chi_{ij}\biggr\rvert

By Azuma-Hoeffding’s bound

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\bigl\lvert\hat{x}_{i}-x_{i}\bigr\rvert>t\right]\leq 2\textrm{exp}\left\{{-\frac{2t^{2}}{\left\lVert{x^{h(i)}}\right\rVert^{2}}}\right\}

Choose
t=\frac{\sqrt{24}\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}}{\sqrt{C}}
.
Define the event

\text{SmallDeviation}(i):~~\bigl\lvert\hat{x}_{i}-x_{i}\bigr\rvert\leq\frac{\sqrt{24}\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}}{\sqrt{C}}

Conditioned on
GoodBucketNorm
(i)
,

\text{Pr}\left[\emph{SmallDeviation}(i)\mid\emph{GoodBucketNorm}(i)\right]\geq 1-2e^{-6}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\emph{SmallDeviation}(i),\emph{GoodBucketNorm}(i)\right]\geq(1-2e^{-6})\cdot\frac{7}{8}\geq\frac{3}{4}

as deduced in
[
CCFC04
]
.

Note 1.
that we use Azuma-Hoeffding’s bound to obtain constant confidence of the form
1-2e^{-6}
, and hence the same can be obtained using a
d
th moment method for even and constant
d
. This in turn requires only
d=O(1)
-wise independence of the Rademacher variables and the hash function.

Note 2.
We intend to use the analysis for sketches involving complex roots of unity and not Rademacher variables. The Azuma-Hoeffding inequality can be applied for each of the real and complex part separately, since each of them are zero mean (since,
\mathbf{E}\left[\omega\right]=0
). The constant factor increases by a factor of 2.

We now return to the question posed earlier in the section. Let
S_{k}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}
be a fixed given set of items from
[n]
. Suppose the items in
[n]
are populated as follows. First, say all items in
[n]\setminus S_{k}
are inserted into the table. Next, the items
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}
are inserted one by one incrementally. Say that the state of
i_{1}
is good (i.e, 1) if
GoodEstimate
(i_{1})
holds and not good (i.e., 0) otherwise. Similarly, after the insertion of
i_{1}
, when
i_{2}
is inserted, the state of
i_{2}
can be either 1 or 0, and so on. Let
s^{j}\in\{0,1\}^{j}
denote the state vector such that
s^{j}_{t}
is 1 iff the state of
i_{t}
is good and 0 otherwise, for
t\in[j]
. We wish to consider the probability

\text{Pr}\left[\emph{GoodEstimate}(i)\mid s^{j}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Define the event
Isolation
(j+1)
to mean that
i_{j+1}
does not collide with
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
. This happens with probability at least
1-\frac{t}{C}\geq 19/20
, since it is assumed that
j+1\leq k\leq C/20
.
Therefore, by union bound,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\emph{GoodBucketNorm}(i_{j+1}),\emph{Isolation}(i_{j+1})\mid s^{j}\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{20}-\frac{1}{8}=\frac{33}{40}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\emph{GoodEstimate}(i_{j+1})\mid s^{j}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\text{Pr}\left[\emph{SmallDeviation}(i_{j+1}),\emph{GoodBucketNorm}(i_{j+1}),\emph{Isolation}(i_{j+1})\mid s^{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Pr}\left[\emph{SmallDeviation}(i_{j+1})\mid\emph{GoodBucketNorm}(i_{j+1}),\emph{Isolation}(i_{j+1}),s^{j}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\cdot\text{Pr}\left[\emph{GoodBucketNorm}(i_{j+1}),\emph{Isolation}(i_{j+1})\mid s^{j}\right]

\displaystyle\geq(1-e^{-6})(33/40)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4}{5}\kern 5.0pt.

We have thus shown that the probability that the item
i_{j+1}\in S_{k}
satisfies
GoodEstimate
is at least
4/5
, no matter what the state of the items
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
may be.

We can now introduce
s
independent copies of the hash function
h
as
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}
, and corresponding tables
T_{1},\ldots,T_{s}
, where, the hash function
h_{r}
is used for table
T_{r}
,
r\in[s]
. Let
\sigma^{j}=(\{0,1\}^{j})^{s}
be the state vector for the state of items
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
in each of the tables
T_{1},\ldots,T_{s}
. That is, the
j
-bit vector
\sigma^{j}_{1\ldots j}
represents the state of items
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
in
T_{1}
, and in general, the
j
-bit segment
\sigma^{j}_{(r-1)j+1,\ldots,rj}
represents the state of the items
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
in table
T_{r}
, for
r=1,2,\ldots,s
.
Denote by
GoodEstimate
(r,i_{j})
the event that in table
T_{r}
, the item
i_{j}
satisfies the event
GoodEstimate
, that is,
\bigl\lvert\hat{x}_{i}-x_{j}\bigr\rvert<\sqrt{\frac{24}{C}}\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}
.
By independence of the hash functions
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}
, for any
r\in[s]
,

\text{Pr}\left[\emph{GoodEstimate}(r,i_{j+1})\mid\sigma^{j}\right]=\text{Pr}\left[\emph{GoodEstimate}(r,i_{j+1})\mid\sigma^{j}_{(r-1)j+1,\ldots,rj}\right]\geq 4/5

For any
j\in[k]
, let
G_{j}
denote the number of tables in which the bucket to which
i_{j}
maps provides a good estimate, that is,

G_{j}=\bigl\lvert\{r\in[s]\mid\emph{GoodEstimate}(i_{j})\text{ holds }\}\bigr\rvert

For analysis purposes, let
\sigma^{j}
denote the state of the buckets to which items
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
maps in each of the tables
T_{1},\ldots,T_{s}
. Let

G_{j+1\mid\sigma^{j}}=\bigl\lvert\{r\in[s]\mid\emph{GoodEstimate}(i_{j+1})\text{ holds conditional on state being }\sigma^{j}\}\bigr\rvert

By the above calculation, the probability that
GoodEstimate
(i_{j+1})
holds conditional on the state being any
\sigma^{j}
is at least
4/5
.
What is the probability that the
CountSketch
estimate, that is,
median
{}_{r=1}^{s}\hat{x}_{r,i_{j}}
is not a good estimate? This is the probability
\text{Pr}\left[G_{j+1}\leq s/2\mid\sigma^{j}\right]
. Let
g_{r,j+1}
be the indicator variable that is 1 iff
GoodEstimate
(i_{j+1})
holds
conditional on the state being
\sigma^{j}
. Then,

G_{j+1}=\sum_{r=1}^{s}g_{r,j+1}

Now,
\text{Pr}\left[g_{r,j+1}\mid\sigma^{j}\right]\geq 4/5
. By Chernoff’s bounds,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[G_{j+1}<s/2\mid\sigma^{j}\right]\leq\textrm{exp}\left\{{-\Theta(s)}\right\},~~~~\text{ for any feasible $\sigma^{j}$},j=0,\ldots,k-1

Let
E_{j}
be the union of any arbitrary states
\sigma^{j}
, that is,
E_{j}=\sigma^{j,1}\vee\ldots\vee\sigma^{j,N}
, for some
N
. For
N=2
,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[g_{r,j+1}\mid\sigma^{j,1}\vee\sigma^{j,2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\text{Pr}\left[g_{r,j+1}\wedge(\sigma^{j,1}\vee\sigma^{j,2})\right]}{\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,1}\vee\sigma^{j,2}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\text{Pr}\left[g_{r,j+1},\sigma^{j,1}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[g_{r,j+1},\sigma^{j,2}\right]}{\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,1}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,2}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\text{Pr}\left[g_{r,j+1}\mid\sigma^{j,1}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,1}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[g_{r,j+1}\mid\sigma^{j,2}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,2}\right]}{\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,1}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,2}\right]}

\displaystyle\geq(4/5)\frac{\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,1}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,2}\right]}{\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,1}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[\sigma^{j,2}\right]}

\displaystyle=(4/5)

Likewise, by induction, one can show that
\text{Pr}\left[g_{r+1}\mid E_{j}\right]\geq 4/5
. Extending the argument, it can be shown that
\text{Pr}\left[G_{j+1}<s/2\mid E_{j}\right]\leq\textrm{exp}\left\{{-\Theta(s)}\right\}
.
It follows that the probability that each of the
k
median estimates is not a good estimate is,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[G_{1}<s/2,G_{2}<s/2,\ldots,G_{k}<s/2\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Pr}\left[G_{k}<s/2\mid G_{k-1}<s/2,\ldots,G_{1}<s/2\right]\cdot\text{Pr}\left[G_{k-1}<s/2\mid G_{k-2}<s/2,\ldots,G_{1}<s/2\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\cdots\cdot\text{Pr}\left[G_{1}<s/2\right]

\displaystyle\leq\textrm{exp}\left\{{-\Theta(s)}\right\}\cdots\textrm{exp}\left\{{-\Theta(s)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\textrm{exp}\left\{{-k\Theta(s)}\right\}

For any set
S_{k}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}
, let the
F_{S_{k}}
be the event that for each
i_{j}\in S
, the estimate
\hat{x}_{i_{j}}
is not a good estimate of
x_{i_{j}}
. Therefore,
\text{Pr}\left[F_{S_{k}}\right]\leq\textrm{exp}\left\{{-k\Theta(s)}\right\}
For any given
k
, and noting that
s=\Theta(\log n)
, we have,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\exists\text{ at least $k$ distinct items whose estimates are not good estimates }\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Pr}\left[\exists S_{k}\subset[n],\lvert S_{k}\rvert=k\text{ s.t. }F_{S_{k}}\text{ holds }\right]

\displaystyle\leq\binom{n}{k}\textrm{exp}\left\{{-k\Theta(s)}\right\}=\textrm{exp}\left\{{-k(\Theta(s)-\log n)}\right\}=\textrm{exp}\left\{{-k\Theta(\log n)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{O(1)}

provided,
k\geq O(\log(1/\delta))/\log(n)
.

In an analogous way, it can be shown that the probability that at most
O(\log(1/\delta))/\log(n)
items may fail to satisfy
nocollision
with probability
1-\delta
.
We have proved the following lemma.

Lemma 55
(Restatement of Lemma
17
.)

With probability
1-O(\delta)
, the number of elements for which
goodest
fails is at most
O(\log(1/\delta))/(\log n)
. With probability
1-O(\delta)
, the number of elements for which
nocollision
fails is
O(\log(1/\delta))/(\log n)
.

B.2
Extending the analysis

Lemma 29 in
[
Gan15
]
can be directly extended to obtain
\bigl\lvert\textsc{discover}_{l}\bigr\rvert\leq 2B_{l}
, with probability
1-\delta^{\Omega(1)}
. Further,
B_{l}=\Omega(C_{L})=\omega(\log(1/\delta))
, with probability
1-\delta n^{-\Omega(1)}
. Using the analysis of Lemma
17
, the number of elements which are discovered (i.e.,
\lvert\hat{x}_{i}\rvert\geq Q_{l}
) and for which
goodest
fails is
O(\log(1/\delta)/\log n)
with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
. Hence,
\bigl\lvert\textsc{discover}_{l}\bigr\rvert\leq 2B_{l}+O(\log(1/\delta)/\log n\leq 2B_{l}+o(B_{L}/\log n)\leq 3B_{l}
, with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
.

We now consider the effect of items in
\left(\text{lmargin}(G_{0})\cup_{l=1}^{L}G_{l}\right)\setminus\textsc{gooditems}_{1}
. This set contains items that for reasons such as, non-discovery, or discovery followed by collisions, get dropped and their scaled contribution to
\hat{F}_{p}
are not added. These also include items that were mistakenly discovered and added to samples. This is because
accuest
holds only with
1-n^{-\Omega(1)}
probability. Let
\text{Error}^{\textsc{ghss}}
denote the total contribution to
\hat{F}_{p}
of such items that are either dropped or erroneously estimated and misclassified within in the
ghss
structure. Let
\text{Error}^{\textsc{shelf}}
denote the total contribution to
\hat{F}_{p}
of items that are either dropped or erroneously misclassified within the shelf structure.

Note
that it is possible for an item to belong to some group
G_{l}
as per the
ghss
grouping, but the item’s frequency could be significantly over-estimated so that it is classified into one of shelves. The error due to over-estimation of an item that belongs to a
ghss
group but gets classified into a shelf sample is calculated when we analyze the error in the estimation from the shelf structure.

Let
\textsc{gooditems}_{1}
be the set of items that satisfy
\textsc{nocollision}\cap\textsc{accuest}
.

Lemma 56
( Restatement of Lemma
18
.)

\text{Error}^{\textsc{ghss}}\leq O\left(\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log n}\right)
with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
.

Proof:

Consider the set
D_{1}\subset\left(\text{lmargin}(G_{0})\cup_{l=1}^{L}G_{l}\right)\setminus\textsc{gooditems}_{1}
consisting of legitimate items that are dropped due to error in estimation or due to collisions. Let
D_{2}\subset\left(\text{lmargin}(G_{0})\cup_{l=1}^{L}G_{l}\right)\setminus\textsc{gooditems}_{1}
consisting of items that are incorrectly discovered or misclassified due to error in estimation.

If
i\in D_{1}\cap G_{l}
then,

\lvert x_{i}\rvert\leq T_{l-1}
, if
l\geq 1
, or otherwise,
\lvert x_{i}\rvert\leq T_{0}(1+\bar{\epsilon})
, where,
\bar{\epsilon}=1/(54p)
. Suppose
i\in D_{2}
and
i\in G_{l^{\prime}}
. However, due to error in estimation, suppose that
i\in\bar{G_{l}}
, for some
l<l^{\prime}
, causing an over-estimate. Such items are in
D_{2}
. For
l\geq 1
, the estimate for
\lvert x_{i}\rvert
, namely,
\lvert X_{i}\rvert
is bounded by
T_{l-1}(1+\bar{\epsilon})
, otherwise, the estimate is dropped from
\bar{G_{l}}
.
If
i\in\bar{G}_{0}
, the upper bound is
U_{1}(1+\bar{\epsilon})
, as is defined in the shelf structure. Here
U_{1}\leq 2^{1/p}T_{0}
.

We also have,

T_{0}=O\left(\frac{F_{2}}{n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-4/p}\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)}\right)^{1/2}\leq O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{4}/pF_{p}^{2/p}}{\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)}\right)^{1/2}\leq O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)^{1/p}

Therefore,
T_{0}^{p}\leq O(\epsilon^{2}F_{p}/\log(1/\delta))
. Further,

T_{l}^{p}\leq O\left(\displaystyle(2\alpha)^{-lp/2}\epsilon^{2}F_{p}/\log(1/\delta)\right)
.
Then, the error contribution due to under-estimation or dropping of items is bounded above as follows.

\displaystyle\text{Error}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in D_{1},i\in\text{lmargin}(G_{0})}T_{0}^{p}(1+\bar{\epsilon})^{p}+\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in D_{1},i\in G_{l}}2^{l}T_{l-1}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)\left(\bigl\lvert D_{1}\cap\text{lmargin}(G_{0})\bigr\rvert+\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l}(2\alpha)^{-lp/2}\bigl\lvert D_{1}\cap G_{l}\bigr\rvert\right)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)\bigl\lvert D_{1}\cap\left(\text{lmargin}(G_{0})\cup_{l=1}^{L-1}G_{l}\right)\bigr\rvert\left(1+\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l}(2\alpha)^{-lp/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log n}\right)\left(1+\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l}(2\alpha)^{-lp/2}\right)

(135)

since, as argued above, the total number of items dropped or mis-estimated is at most
O(\log(1/\delta)/\log n)
.

Recall that
\alpha=1-(1-2/p)\nu
, for a small constant
\nu
. Let
\gamma=(1-2/p)\nu
so that
\alpha=1-\gamma
. Therefore,
\ln\alpha=\ln(1-\gamma)\geq-2\gamma
.
Now,

(2\alpha)^{-p/2}=2^{-(p/2)\log_{2}(2\alpha)}=2^{-(p/2)(1+\ln\alpha/\ln 2)}\leq 2^{-(p/2)(1-\gamma(2/\ln 2))}\kern 5.0pt.

Since,
(p/2)\gamma=(p/2-1)\nu
, therefore,

2(2\alpha)^{-p/2}=2^{1-(p/2)(1-\gamma(2/\ln 2))}=2^{(1-p/2)+(p/2-1)\nu(2/\ln 2)}=2^{(1-p/2)(1-2\nu/\ln 2)}

The value
2^{(1-p/2)(1-2\nu/\ln 2)}
is a constant
<1
since,
p/2>1
and
\nu=0.01<\ln 2/2
.
Thus, we have,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l}(2\alpha)^{-lp/2}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\left(\frac{2}{(2\alpha)^{p/2}}\right)^{l}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l(1-p/2)(1-2\nu/\ln 2)}=\Theta(1)

Substituting this in Eqn. (
B.2
), we have,

\text{Error}_{1}\leq O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log n}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The error contribution of the items in
D_{2}
is bounded as follows.

\displaystyle\text{Error}_{2}=\sum_{i\in D_{2}\cap\bar{G_{0}}}(U_{1}(1+\bar{\epsilon}))^{p}+\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in D_{2},i\in G_{l}}2^{l}T_{l-1}^{p}

This sum is bounded similarly, since
U_{1}\leq 2^{1/p}T_{0}
, and therefore,
\text{Error}_{2}\leq O\left(\epsilon^{2}F_{p}/\log n\right)
.

Therefore, combining,
\text{Error}^{\textsc{ghss}}=\text{Error}_{1}+\text{Error}_{2}\leq O\left(\epsilon^{2}F_{p}/\log n\right)
.

Appendix C
Analysis of Shelf Structure

As discussed in Section
3
, the shelf structure is needed when
\log(1/\delta)=\omega(\log(n))
.
In this section, we will assume that
\log(1/\delta)=\omega(\log(n))
. Further, as discussed,
we will emphasize the interesting case when
H_{J}=o(H_{0})
.

Define the following two constraints on the parameters
a
and
b
.

\displaystyle(1)~~\lvert\ln(ab)\rvert=\Omega(1)~~~~~\text{ and }~~~~(2)~~b=\Omega(1)\kern 5.0pt.

(136)

Constraint 1 is derived from the following consideration. The number of measurements required by the
j
th shelf is
O(H_{j}w_{j})
.

Case 1.
Suppose
ab<1
. Then, by constraint (1),
1-ab=1-\textrm{exp}\left\{{-\lvert\ln ab\rvert}\right\}=1-O(1)=\Omega(1)
. The sum of the number of measurements required by all the shelves is

\sum_{j=0}^{J}H_{j}w_{j}=H_{0}w_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{J}(ab)^{j}\leq\frac{H_{0}w_{0}}{(1-ab)}=O(H_{0}w_{0})\kern 5.0pt.

Case 2.
Suppose
ab>1
. Then, by constraint (1),
1-(ab)^{-1}=1-\textrm{exp}\left\{{-\lvert\ln ab\rvert}\right\}=1-O(1)=\Omega(1)
. The sum of the number of measurements across all shelves becomes

\sum_{j=0}^{J}H_{j}w_{j}=H_{J}w_{J}\sum_{k=0}^{J}(ab)^{-k}\leq\frac{H_{J}w_{J}}{1-(ab)^{-1}}=O(H_{J}w_{J})\kern 5.0pt.

So, by constraint 1, in either case, the total space used by the shelf structure is
O(H_{0}w_{0}+H_{J}w_{J})
.
The motivation for Constraint 2 arises from the need to bound the
Error
{}^{\textsc{shelf}}
term, as in the proof of Lemma
18
. We now outline how
a
and
b
can be chosen to satisfy constraints 1 and 2.

We have,
a^{J}=w_{J}/w_{0}=\Theta(\ln(1/\delta)/(\ln n))=\omega(1)
and
b^{J}=H_{J}/H_{0}=o(1)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(ab)^{J}

\displaystyle=\frac{w_{J}}{w_{0}}\cdot\frac{H_{J}}{H_{0}}=\Theta(1)\cdot\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\log n}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{-2}}{\epsilon^{-4/p}\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)}=\Theta(1)\cdot\frac{\left(\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta)\right)^{1-2/p}}{\log n}

Let

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=\ln\left(H_{J}w_{J}/(H_{0}w_{0})\right)=J\ln(ab)\kern 5.0pt.

Let
J=L_{0}
so that
\ln(ab)=1
or
ab=e
. Set
b=1/2
and therefore
a=2e
.

This shows that
a
and
b
can be chosen to satisfy constraints 1 and 2.

C.1
Error Analysis for Shelf Structure

We first extend the definition of events
\textsc{accuest}_{1}
,
\textsc{smallhh}_{1}
and
\textsc{nocollision}_{1}
to the shelf structure. Let
\textsc{gooditems}_{1}=\textsc{nocollision}_{1}\cap\textsc{smallhh}_{1}\cap\textsc{nocollision}_{1}
. We first estimate the error arising in the estimate
\hat{F}_{p}^{\textsc{shelf}}
due to items with frequency in the range
[T_{0},\infty)

\setminus\textsc{gooditems}_{1}
, with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
. That is, these items were either dropped on account of collision, or due to inaccurate estimation, or they were misclassified into a larger shelf, also due to inaccurate estimation.

Suppose
i
belongs to the frequency range of shelf
j
. Then, the probability of
\textsc{nocollision}(i)
failing, or
\textsc{accuest}(i)
failing or
\textsc{smallhh}_{j}
failing is at most
e^{-\Omega(w_{j})}
. In particular, since,
w_{J}=\Theta(\log(1/\delta))
, it follows that items belonging to the range of shelf numbered
J
satisfy
accuest
and
smallhh
and
nocollision
, with probability
1-\delta^{\Omega(1)}
, and therefore, there is no contribution to error from the last shelf up to probability
1-\delta^{\Omega(1)}
.

Lemma 57
(Restatement of first part of Lemma
18
.)

Assuming constraints 1 and 2,

\displaystyle\text{Error}^{\textsc{shelf}}\leq\max\left(O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log n}\right),O(\epsilon^{p}F_{p})\right)

with probability
1-\delta/n^{\Omega(1)}
.

Proof:

For shelf index
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,J-1\}
, let
D^{(j)}
denote the set of items that belong to the frequency range of shelf indexed
j
but do not belong to
\textsc{gooditems}_{1}
. Let
\lvert D^{(j)}\rvert=d_{j}
. The contribution to the error term from these items is at most
U_{j+1}^{p}d_{j}
. Further, by the calculation in Lemma
17
,
d_{j}\leq O\left(\log(1/\delta)/w_{j}\right)
. More generally, from Lemma
17
, we have that
\sum_{j=0}^{J}w_{j}d_{j}=O(\log(1/\delta))
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\text{Error}^{\textsc{shelf}}\leq

\displaystyle\text{ Maximize}\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}U_{j+1}^{p}d_{j}\text{ subject to }\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}w_{j}d_{j}=O(\log(1/\delta))

(137)

This is a linear program with feasible region
\sum_{j=0}^{J-1}w_{j}d_{j}=O(\log(1/\delta))
and
d_{j}\geq 0
, for
j=0,\ldots,J-1
. The optimal value of this linear program lies on a vertex of the corresponding polygonal face in
J
-dimensional space
{\mathbb{R}}^{J}
. A vertex of this face is of the form
\hat{d}_{j-1}e_{j}=(0,\ldots,0,\hat{d}_{j-1},0,\ldots,0)^{T}
, where,
\hat{d}_{j-1}=O(\log(1/\delta))/w_{j}
, for each
j=0,1,\ldots,J-1
. Here,
e_{j}
is the
j
th column of the
J\times J
identity matrix.

We have,

U_{j+1}^{p}=\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}b^{j+1}}\right)^{p/2}\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)b^{-(j+1)(p/2)}

The objective value at the vertex
\hat{d}_{j}e_{j}=(0,\ldots,0,\hat{d}_{j},0,\ldots,0)
is

\displaystyle U_{j+1}^{p}\hat{d}_{j}\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)b^{-(j+1)(p/2)}\left(\frac{O(\log(1/\delta))}{w_{j}}\right)=\left(\frac{O(\epsilon^{2}F_{p})}{b^{p/2}\log(n)}\right)\left(\frac{1}{(ab^{p/2})^{j}}\right)

(138)

using
w_{j}=\Theta(\log n)a^{j}
.

As has been discussed, we can choose the parameters
a
and
b
, so that
\lvert\ln(ab)\rvert=\Omega(1)
and
b=\Omega(1)
. Therefore,
b^{p/2}=\Omega(1)
.

Case 1:
ab^{p/2}<1
. By Eqn. (
138
), the vertex maximizing the objective function occurs at
\hat{d}_{J-1}e_{J}
, that is, the vertex corresponding to
j=J-1
. Then,

\displaystyle(ab^{p/2})^{J}

\displaystyle=(w_{J}/w_{0})(H_{j}/H_{0})^{p/2}=\left(\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\log(n)}\right)\left(\frac{\epsilon^{-2+4/p}}{\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)}\right)^{p/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\log(n)}\right)\left(\frac{\epsilon^{-(p-2)}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)=\frac{\epsilon^{-(p-2)}}{\log n}\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore, the maximum objective value is

\displaystyle U_{J}^{p}\hat{d}_{J}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{O(\epsilon^{2}F_{p})}{b^{p/2}\log(n)}\right)\left(\frac{1}{(ab^{p/2})^{J}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{O(\epsilon^{2})F_{p}}{b^{p/2}\log(n)}\right)\left(\epsilon^{p-2}\log n\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\epsilon^{p}b^{-p/2}F_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\epsilon^{p}F_{p}\right)

assuming
b=\Omega(1)
.

Case 2:
ab^{p/2}>1
.
Then the vertex maximizing the objective value occurs at
\hat{d}_{0}e_{1}
, that is corresponding to
j=0
. This value is

\displaystyle U_{1}^{p}\hat{d}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{O(\epsilon^{2}F_{p})}{b^{p/2}\log(n)}\right)=\frac{O(\epsilon^{2}F_{p})}{\log n}

assuming
b=\Omega(1)
.

Case 3:
ab^{p/2}=1
.
In this case, all vertices have the same objective value, which is
\frac{O(\epsilon^{2}F_{p})}{\log n}
. This proves the Lemma.

Lemma 58
(Expanded Restatement of Lemma
19
.)

Let
1\leq e,g\leq\lceil\log(1/\delta)\rceil
and
l\in S_{j}
. Let
\log(1/\delta)=\omega(\log n)
. Assume that
\textsc{accuest}(l)
holds and
H_{j}\geq\Omega(p^{2}E_{J})
and
\lvert x_{l}\rvert\geq\left(\frac{F_{2}}{E_{j}}\right)^{1/2}
. Let the family
\{\omega_{lr}(i)\}_{i\in[n]}
be
O(log(1/\delta))
-wise independent . Then,
\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\left(1+\frac{Z_{l}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{e}\left(\left(1+\frac{\overline{Z_{l}}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{g}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]
is real and non-negative and is bounded above by
\displaystyle c^{h}\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{-h}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j}}\right)^{h/2}\left(\min\left(\frac{h}{w_{j}},1\right)\right)^{h/2}
,
where,
h=e+g
and
c
is an absolute constant.
Further,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(Y_{l}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{l}\right]\right)^{e}\left(\overline{Y_{l}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{l}}\right]\right)^{g}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq c^{h}\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{(p-1)h}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j}}\right)^{h/2}\left(\min\left(\frac{h}{w_{j}},1\right)\right)^{h/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Note.
It suffices to condition on the conjunction of events
\mathcal{G}\wedge\textsc{nocollision}(l)\wedge\textsc{goodest}(l)
instead of
\mathcal{G}\wedge\textsc{nocollision}\wedge\textsc{goodest}
.

Proof:
Following the initial part of the proof of Lemma
12
, we have,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\left(1+\frac{Z_{l}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{e}\left(\left(1+\frac{\overline{Z_{l}}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{g}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1}+\ldots+a_{k}+\ldots=e}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b_{1}+\ldots+b_{k}+\ldots=g\\
\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}=\sum_{s\geq 1}sb_{s}\end{subarray}}c_{1}^{e+g}\prod_{r\geq 1}\left(\frac{c_{r}}{c_{1}}\right)^{a_{r}}\prod_{s\geq 1}\left(\frac{c_{s}}{c_{1}}\right)^{b_{s}}\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[(Z\overline{Z})^{\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{2\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}}

(139)

Case 1
:
h/2\leq\Theta(w_{j})
.

Following earlier calculation,
for non-zero expectation, we have to assume that
\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}=\sum_{s\geq 1}sb_{s}
. Thus, we have,

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[(Z_{l}\overline{Z_{l}})^{\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{2\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[(Z_{l}\overline{Z_{l}})^{h/2}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{h}}\left(\frac{\lvert Z_{l}\rvert}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{2(\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r})-h}

\displaystyle\leq\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{-h}\left(\frac{hF_{2}}{\Theta(w_{j})H_{j}}\right)^{h/2}\varrho^{2(\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r})-h}

Again, following analogous calculation in Lemma
12
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\left(1+\frac{Z_{l}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{e}\left(\left(1+\frac{\overline{Z_{l}}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{g}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{-h}c^{\prime h}\left(\frac{hF_{2}}{O(w_{j})H_{j}}\right)^{h/2}

(140)

for some constant
c^{\prime}
.

Case 2
:
h/2>\Theta(w_{j})
. Let
\Theta(w_{j})=w^{\prime}_{j}
.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[(Z_{l}\overline{Z_{l}})^{\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{2\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[(Z_{l}\overline{Z_{l}})^{w^{\prime}_{j}}\mid H\right]}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{2w^{\prime}_{j}}}\varrho^{2(\sum_{r}ra_{r})-h}\left(\frac{\lvert Z_{l}\rvert}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{h-2w^{\prime}_{j}}\varrho^{2(\sum_{r}ra_{r})-h}

(141)

Now
\mathbf{E}\left[(Z_{l}\overline{Z_{l}})^{w^{\prime}_{j}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\left(\frac{w^{\prime}_{j}F_{2}}{w^{\prime}_{j}H_{j}}\right)^{w^{\prime}_{j}}
, and by
accuest
(l)
,
\lvert Z_{l}\rvert\leq\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j}}\right)^{1/2}
. Substituting in Eqn. (
141
), we have,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[(Z_{l}\overline{Z_{l}})^{\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{2\sum_{r\geq 1}ra_{r}}}

\displaystyle\leq\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{-h_{j}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j}}\right)^{h_{j}/2}\varrho^{2\sum_{r}ra_{r}-h}

From here, the proof may proceed along the lines of Lemma
12
. This yields,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\left(1+\frac{Z_{l}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{e}\left(\left(1+\frac{\overline{Z_{l}}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{g}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{-h}c^{\prime h}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j}}\right)^{h/2}

for some constant
c^{\prime}
.

Combining cases 1 and 2, we have in general that,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\left(1+\frac{Z_{l}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{e}\left(\left(1+\frac{\overline{Z_{l}}}{\lvert x_{l}\rvert}\right)^{p}-1\right)^{g}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\lvert x_{l}\rvert^{-h}c^{\prime h}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j}}\right)^{h/2}\min\left(\frac{h}{w_{j}},1\right)^{h/2}\kern 5.0pt.

The remainder of the proof proceeds identically along the lines of the proof of Lemma
12
.

We will decompose the two statements of Lemma
20
into two lemmas, one corresponding to the contribution to the
2d
th central moment from all the shelves except the outermost shelf, and the second corresponding to the contribution to the same from the outermost shelf. We prove them separately and then combine the results. Let
S^{\prime}=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{J-1}
.

Lemma 59

Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}\leq\log(1/\delta)
and integral. Then, there exists an absolute constant
c
such that

\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\right)^{d_{1}}\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\overline{\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right]})\right)^{d_{2}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\left(c\epsilon F_{p}\right)^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma 60

Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}\leq\log(1/\delta)
and integral. Then, there exists an absolute constant
c
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\right)^{d_{1}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\overline{\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right]})\right)^{d_{2}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\left(c\epsilon F_{p}\right)^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof:
[Proof of Lemma
59
.] Consider the case when say
d_{1}=0
and
d_{2}>0
. Then, the expression in the expectation is

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\overline{\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right]})\right)^{d_{2}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{d_{2}}\sum_{e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=d_{2}}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S^{\prime}}\prod_{u=1}^{r}\mathbf{E}\left[(\overline{Y_{i}}-\overline{\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right]})^{e_{u}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{d_{2}}\sum_{e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=d_{2}}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S^{\prime}}\prod_{u=1}^{r}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{e_{u}}n^{-\Omega(e_{u})}

\displaystyle\leq F_{p}^{d_{2}}n^{-\Omega(d_{2})}

which proves the claim. The proof for
d_{2}=0
and
d_{1}>0
is analogous.

The
LHS
of the expression in the lemma may be written as follows. This is the same expression as in Eqn. (
A.7
) in the proof of Lemma
14
. Let
\alpha_{0}(q)=\max(0,1-q)
. Using
nocollision
and the argument in Lemma
14

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\right)^{d_{1}}\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\overline{\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right]})\right)^{d_{2}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{\min(d_{1},d_{2})}\sum_{s=\alpha_{0}(q)}^{d_{1}-q}\sum_{t=\alpha_{0}(q)}^{d_{2}-q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q+s}=d_{1}\\
e_{1},\ldots,e_{q+s}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}+\ldots+g_{q+s}=d_{2}\\
g_{1},\ldots,g_{q+t}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q},j_{1},\ldots,j_{s},k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}\}\subset[n]}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\prod_{r=1}^{q}\mathbf{E}\left[(Y_{i_{r}}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i_{r}}\right])^{e_{r}}\left(\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}\right]\right)^{g_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\prod_{l=1}^{s}\mathbf{E}\left[(Y_{j_{l}}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{j_{l}}\right])^{e_{q+l}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\prod_{m=1}^{t}\mathbf{E}\left[(Y_{k_{m}}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{k_{m}}\right])^{e_{q+m}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(142)

Analogous to the definitions of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
in Eqn. (
A.7
), define

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
q=0\text{iff}s_{1}=0\end{subarray}}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=s_{1}}\sum_{g_{1}+\ldots+g_{q}=s_{2}}\binom{s_{1}}{e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}}\binom{s_{2}}{g_{1},\ldots,g_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{r=1}^{q}\mathbf{E}\left[(Y_{i_{r}}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])^{e_{r}}\left(\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\right)^{g_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(143)

\displaystyle\beta(d_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=\alpha_{0}(s_{1})}^{d_{1}-s_{1}}\sum_{e_{q+1}+\ldots+e_{q+s}=d_{1}-s_{1}}\binom{d_{1}-s_{1}}{e_{q+1},\ldots,e_{q+s}}\sum_{\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}\}}\prod_{l=1}^{s}\lvert x_{j_{l}}\rvert^{pe_{q+l}}n^{-\Omega(e_{q+l})}

\displaystyle=\left(n^{-\Omega(1)}F_{p}\right)^{d_{1}-s_{1}}\kern 5.0pt.

(144)

Following the calculations of Lemma
14
, the expression in Eqn. (
C.1
) is bounded above as

\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{d_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}=0}^{d_{2}}\binom{d_{1}}{s_{1}}\binom{d_{2}}{s_{2}}\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})\beta(d_{1},s_{1})\beta(d_{2},s_{2})\\
\leq\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{d_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}=0}^{d_{2}}\binom{d_{1}}{s_{1}}\binom{d_{2}}{s_{2}}\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})\left(n^{-\Omega(1)}F_{p}\right)^{d_{1}+d_{2}-s_{1}+s_{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

(145)

We now make the following claim.

Claim 61

\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})\leq(c^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}
for some constant
c^{\prime}
.

Using this claim, we have from Eqn. (
145
) that this expression is bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{d_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}=0}^{d_{2}}\binom{d_{1}}{s_{1}}\binom{d_{2}}{s_{2}}(c^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(n^{-\Omega(1)}F_{p}\right)^{d_{1}+d_{2}-s_{1}+s_{2}}=\left((c^{\prime}\epsilon+n^{-\Omega(1)})F_{p}\right)^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

thereby proving the statement of the Lemma.

For the remainder of the proof, it will suffice to prove
Claim
61
. Let
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
be each non-zero, otherwise, the sum is vacuous.

For
i_{u}\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup\ldots\cup S_{J-1}
, let
j(i_{u})
denote the index
j
of the shelf
S_{j}
such that
i\in S_{j}
. From Lemma
19
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=s_{1}\\
e_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}+\ldots+g_{q}=s_{2}\\
g_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}}{e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}}\binom{s_{2}}{g_{1},\ldots,g_{q}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{r=1}^{q}\mathbf{E}\left[(Y_{i_{r}}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])^{e_{r}}\left(\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\right)^{g_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=s_{1}\\
e_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}+\ldots+g_{q}=s_{2}\\
g_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}}{e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}}\binom{s_{2}}{g_{1},\ldots,g_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p-1)h_{u}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j(i_{u})}}\right)^{h_{u}/2}\left(\min\left(\frac{h_{u}}{w_{j(i_{u})}},1\right)\right)^{h_{u}/2},

(146)

where,
h_{u}=e_{u}+g_{u}
.

By definition of multinomial coefficients, we have,

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p-1)h_{u}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j(i_{u})}}\right)^{h_{u}/2}\left(\min\left(\frac{h_{u}}{w_{j(i_{u})}},1\right)\right)^{h_{u}c_{0}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}/2}\kern 5.0pt.

(147)

For
i_{u}\in s_{j(i_{u})}
, where,
j(i_{u})\in\{0,1,\ldots,J-1\}
, it follows that
\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert=\Theta((F_{2}/H_{j(i_{u})})^{1/2})
. Therefore, for
\beta=p/2-1
, we have,

\displaystyle\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p-1)h_{u}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j(i_{u})}}\right)^{h_{u}/2}\leq c_{1}^{h_{u}}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p/2)h_{u}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j(i_{u})}}\right)^{ph_{u}/4}

for some constant
c_{1}
.
Further, by the definition of
H_{j}=H_{0}b^{j}
, we have,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j(i_{u})}}\right)^{ph_{u}/4}=\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{ph_{u}/4}b^{-j(i_{u})ph_{u}/4}\kern 5.0pt.

(148)

Now, for any
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,J\}
,

b^{-j}=b^{-J(j/J)}=\left(\frac{H_{0}}{H_{J}}\right)^{j/J}=\left(\epsilon^{2-4/p}\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)\right)^{j/J}\leq\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)^{(2/p)(j/J)}\kern 5.0pt.

Substituting this in Eqn. (
148
) and then in Eqn. (
C.1
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{j(i_{u})}}\right)^{ph_{u}/4}\leq\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{ph_{u}/4}\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)^{jh_{u}/(2J)}\kern 5.0pt.

Further,

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}c_{1}^{h_{u}}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p/2)h_{u}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{ph_{u}/4}\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)^{(j(i_{u})h_{u}/(2J)}\left(\min\left(\frac{h_{u}}{w_{j(i_{u})}},1\right)\right)^{h_{u}/2}

\displaystyle=(c_{0}c_{1})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{p(s_{1}+s_{2})/4}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p/2)h_{u}}\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)^{(j(i_{u})h_{u}/(2J)}\left(\min\left(\frac{h_{u}}{w_{j(i_{u})}},1\right)\right)^{h_{u}/2}\kern 5.0pt.

(149)

Consider the product
(\log(1/\delta))^{j(i_{u})/J}\min((h_{u}/w_{j(i_{u})}),1)
and suppose that we wish to maximize it as a function of
j(i_{u})
. First suppose that
h_{u}\geq w_{j(i_{u})}
. Then, the product is simply
(\log(1/\delta))^{j(i_{u})/J}
and increases with increasing
j(i_{u})
. Thus, in this case, the maximum of the product is
\log(1/\delta)
. Now suppose
h_{u}<w_{j(i_{u})}
. For simplicity, let
j_{u}
denote
j(i_{u})
.
Incrementing
j_{u}
by 1, and noting that
w_{j+1}/w_{j}=a
and
a^{J}O(\log(n))=\log(1/\delta)
, consider the ratio

\displaystyle\frac{(\log(1/\delta))^{(j_{u}+1)/J}(h_{u}/w_{j_{u}+1})}{(\log(1/\delta))^{j_{u}/J}(h_{u}/w_{j_{u}})}=(\log(1/\delta))^{1/J}a^{-1}=(O(\log n))^{1/J}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, the ratio increases by incrementing
j(i_{u})
. Hence, in both cases, the maximum is obtained when
j{i_{u}}=J-1
, that is,
w_{j(i_{u})}=w_{J-1}=O(\log(1/\delta)
. Substituting in Eqn. (
C.1
), we have,

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

(150)

\displaystyle\leq(c_{0}c_{1})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{p(s_{1}+s_{2})/4}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p/2)h_{u}}\left(\log(1/\delta)\right)^{(J-1/J)h_{u}/2}\left(\frac{h_{u}}{O(\log(1/\delta))}\right)^{h_{u}/2}

\displaystyle\leq(c_{0}c_{1}c_{2})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{p(s_{1}+s_{2})/4}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}h_{u}^{h_{u}/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p/2)h_{u}}\kern 5.0pt.

(151)

Now

\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{p/2}=\left(\frac{\epsilon^{4/p}F_{p}^{2/p}}{\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)}\right)^{p/2}=\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore,
\displaystyle\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{0}}\right)^{p(s_{1}+s_{2})/4}\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}
.

Further, since, each
h_{u}\geq 2
, we have,

\displaystyle\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}h_{u}^{h_{u}/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(s_{1}+s_{2})!}{\prod_{u=1}^{q}h_{u}!}\prod_{u=1}^{q}h_{u}^{h_{u}/2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}(s_{1}+s_{2})^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1}/2,\ldots,h_{q}/2}\kern 5.0pt.

for some constant
c_{3}
.
Therefore, Eqn. (
150
) can be written as

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle\leq(c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}(s_{1}+s_{2})^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}

\displaystyle\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}{h_{1}/2,\ldots,h_{q}/2}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}

(152)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p/2)h_{u}}.

(153)

Following the arguments in the proof of Lemma
46
, we can show that

\displaystyle\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}{h_{1}/2,\ldots,h_{q}/2}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p/2)h_{u}}\leq\left(c_{4}F_{p}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\kern 5.0pt.

for some absolute constant
c_{4}
.

Substituting in Eqn. (
150
), we have,

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}F_{p}}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}(s_{1}+s_{2})^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\left(c_{4}F_{p}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(C^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p}\right)^{s_{1}+s_{2}}

assuming
s_{1},s_{2}
are each
O(\log(1/\delta))
, for some absolute constant
C^{\prime}
.

Lemma 62
(Restatement of Lemma
60
.)

Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}\leq\log(1/\delta)
and integral. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\right)^{d_{1}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\overline{\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right]})\right)^{d_{2}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\left(c\epsilon F_{p}\right)^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

for some absolute constant
c
.

Proof:
[Proof of Lemma
60
.]
If
d_{1}=0
and
d_{2}>0
or vice-versa, then, as proved in the initial part of the proof of Lemma
19
, the expectation in the statement of the lemma is bounded above as
(F_{p}n^{-\Omega(1)})^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
, thereby satisfying the statement of the lemma.

So now we assume that both
d_{1},d_{2}>0
. Continuing as in the proof of Lemma
19
, let us define
\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})
, for
1\leq s_{1}\leq d_{1}
and
1\leq s_{2}\leq d_{2}
as in Eqn. (
C.1
), which is reproduced below.

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
q=0\text{iff}s_{1}=0\end{subarray}}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=s_{1}}\sum_{g_{1}+\ldots+g_{q}=s_{2}}\binom{s_{1}}{e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}}\binom{s_{2}}{g_{1},\ldots,g_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S_{J}}\prod_{r=1}^{q}\mathbf{E}\left[(Y_{i_{r}}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])^{e_{r}}\left(\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\right)^{g_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

Following the arguments in the proof of Lemma
19
, to prove the given lemma it suffices to show that
\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})\leq(c^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}
, for some constant
c^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=s_{1}\\
e_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}+\ldots+g_{q}=s_{2}\\
g_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}}{e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}}\binom{s_{2}}{g_{1},\ldots,g_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S_{J}}\prod_{r=1}^{q}\mathbf{E}\left[(Y_{i_{r}}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])^{e_{r}}\left(\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i_{r}}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\right)^{g_{r}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{1}+\ldots+e_{q}=s_{1}\\
e_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}+\ldots+g_{q}=s_{2}\\
g_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 1\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}}{e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}}\binom{s_{2}}{g_{1},\ldots,g_{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S_{J}}\prod_{u=1}^{q}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p-1)h_{u}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{J}}\right)^{h_{u}/2}\left(\frac{h_{u}}{w_{J}}\right)^{h_{u}/2},

\displaystyle\hskip 216.81pt\{h_{u}=e_{u}+g_{u},u\in[q]\}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{J}}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}(c_{2}\log(1/\delta))^{-(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}\prod_{u=1}^{r}h_{u}^{h_{u}/2}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S_{J}}\prod_{u=1}^{r}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p-1)h_{u}}\kern 5.0pt.

Following the arguments in the proof of Lemma
46
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{s_{1}+s_{2}}{h_{1},\ldots,h_{q}}\prod_{u=1}^{r}h_{u}^{h_{u}/2}\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S_{J}}\prod_{u=1}^{r}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(p-1)h_{u}}

\displaystyle\leq(s_{1}+s_{2})^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\sum_{q=1}^{\min(s_{1},s_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+\ldots+h_{q}=s_{1}+s_{2}\\
h_{j}^{\prime}s\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}{h_{1}/2,\ldots,h_{q}/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\sum_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}\}\subset S_{J}}\prod_{u=1}^{r}\lvert x_{i_{u}}\rvert^{(2p-2)h_{u}/2}

\displaystyle\leq(s_{1}+s_{2})^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}c_{3}^{s_{1}+s_{2}}F_{2p-2}^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Substituting in the expression for
\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle\leq(c_{0}c_{2}c_{3})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{J}}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}((s_{1}+s_{2})/\log(1/\delta))^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}F_{2p-2}^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Now
(s_{1}+s_{2})/\log(1/\delta))\leq 2
. Further,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{F_{2}}{H_{J}}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\leq\left(\epsilon^{2}F_{p}^{2/p}\right)^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}=\epsilon^{(s_{1}+s_{2})}F_{p}^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/p}\kern 5.0pt.

Also,

F_{2p-2}^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\leq F_{p}^{(2-2/p)(s_{1}+s_{2})/2}\leq F_{p}^{(1-1/p)(s_{1}+s_{2})}\kern 5.0pt.

Substituting these simplifications, we have,

\displaystyle\alpha(s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle\leq(2c_{0}c_{2}c_{3})^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\epsilon^{(s_{1}+s_{2})}F_{p}^{(s_{1}+s_{2})/p}F_{p}^{(1-1/p)(s_{1}+s_{2})}=\left(c^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p}\right)^{s_{1}+s_{2}}

This implies the statement of the lemma, as argued earlier.

Lemma 63
(Restatement of Lemma
21
.)

Let
\log(1/\delta)=\omega(\log n)
.
Let
C\geq Ln^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-4/p}\allowbreak\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)
and
H_{J}=L^{\prime}n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}
, for suitable constants
L,L^{\prime}
.
Then, for
d=\lceil\log(1/\delta)\rceil
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\right])\right)^{d}\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\right])\right)^{d}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\left(c\epsilon F_{p}\right)^{2d}

for some constant
c
.

Proof:
The proof follows the lines of the proof of Lemma
15
with the modification that we use Lemmas
60
,
20
and
11
instead. We sketch an outline below.
Let
G^{\prime}=\text{lmargin}(G_{0})\cup_{l=1}^{L}G_{l}
. Let
S^{\prime}=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{J-1}
.

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\right)^{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}=d}\binom{d}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\left(\sum_{i\in G^{\prime}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\right)^{d_{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d_{2}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\right)^{d_{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, we expand the conjugate expression
\left(\sum_{i\in[n]\cap\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d}
.

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d^{\prime}_{1}+d^{\prime}_{2}+d^{\prime}_{3}=d}\binom{d}{d^{\prime}_{1}d^{\prime}_{2}d^{\prime}_{3}}\left(\sum_{i\in G^{\prime}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d^{\prime}_{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d^{\prime}_{2}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d^{\prime}_{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

Taking the product and then its expectation, we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in[n]\cap\mathcal{G}_{1}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d}\left(\sum_{i\in[n]\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}=d}\sum_{d^{\prime}_{1}+d^{\prime}_{2}+d^{\prime}_{3}=d}\binom{d}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\binom{d}{d^{\prime}_{1}d^{\prime}_{2}d^{\prime}_{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.22743pt\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in G^{\prime}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d_{1}}\left(\sum_{i\in G^{\prime}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d^{\prime}_{1}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 7.22743pt\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d_{2}}\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d^{\prime}_{2}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 7.22743pt\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d_{3}}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{J}}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d^{\prime}_{3}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

By Lemmas
60
,
20
and
11
, there is some constant
c^{\prime}
such that the above expression is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}=d}\sum_{d^{\prime}_{1}+d^{\prime}_{2}+d^{\prime}_{3}=d}\binom{d}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\binom{d}{d^{\prime}_{1}d^{\prime}_{2}d^{\prime}_{3}}(c^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p})^{d_{1}+d^{\prime}_{1}+d_{2}+d^{\prime}_{2}+d_{3}+d^{\prime}_{3}}

\displaystyle=(c^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p})^{2d}\sum_{d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}=d}\sum_{d^{\prime}_{1}+d^{\prime}_{2}+d^{\prime}_{3}=d}\binom{d}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\binom{d}{d^{\prime}_{1}d^{\prime}_{2}d^{\prime}_{3}}

\displaystyle=(c^{\prime}\epsilon F_{p})^{2d}(1+1+1)^{d}(1+1+1)^{d}

\displaystyle=(c\epsilon F_{p})^{2d}

for some (other) constant
c
.

Lemma 64

For
\log(1/\delta)=\omega(\log n)
,
0<\epsilon<1/2
,
C\geq Ln^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-4/p}\log^{2/p}(1/\delta)
and
H_{J}=L^{\prime}n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}
, for suitable constants
L,L^{\prime}
, then,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\bigl\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\bigr\rvert\leq(\epsilon F_{p})/2\right]\leq\delta/n^{\Theta(1)}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof:

In the statement of Lemma
21
, let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/(8cc^{\prime})
, where,
c^{\prime}\geq 1
is a constant to be chosen below, and choose
L
and
L^{\prime}
appropriately, by increasing them by a proportionate constant factor. Then, by Lemma
21
, for

d=\lceil\log(1/\delta)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d}\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}(\overline{Y_{i}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\overline{Y_{i}}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right)^{d}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\left((\epsilon/(8c^{\prime}))F_{p}\right)^{2d}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\left\lvert\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right\rvert>(\epsilon/2)F_{p}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\sum_{i\in[n]}(Y_{i}-\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right])\right\rvert^{2d}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]}{((\epsilon/2)F_{p})^{2d}}

\displaystyle\leq(4c^{\prime})^{-2\log(1/\delta)}

\displaystyle=\delta^{4+\log c^{\prime}}

From definition,
\mathbf{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid H\right]=\lvert f_{i}\rvert^{p}
, and therefore,
\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[n]}Y_{i}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]=F_{p}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>(\epsilon/2)F_{p}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\delta^{4+\log c^{\prime}}\kern 5.0pt.

Now,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>\epsilon F_{p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>(\epsilon/2)F_{p}\mid\mathcal{H}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\mathcal{H}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>\epsilon F_{p}\mid\neg\mathcal{H}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\neg\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{4}(1-n^{-\Theta(1)})+\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>\epsilon F_{p}\mid\neg\mathcal{H}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\neg\mathcal{H}\right]

(154)

As shown in Lemma
18
, the unaccounted error term, in addition to the error term conditional under
\mathcal{H}
is
\text{Error}^{\textsc{ghss}}+\text{Error}^{\textsc{shelf}}=O(\epsilon^{\prime 2}F_{p}/\log(n)+O(\epsilon^{\prime p}F_{p})\leq 2\epsilon^{{}^{\prime}2}
, with probability
1-\delta
. Let
c^{\prime}=4
.
Thus, conditional on
\mathcal{H}
failing, the error increases
to

(\epsilon/2)F_{p}+2\epsilon^{{}^{\prime}2}F_{p}\leq\epsilon F_{p}

with probability
1-\delta^{O(1)}
by Lemma
18
. Hence,
\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>\epsilon F_{p}\mid\neg\mathcal{H}\right]\leq\delta/n^{\Theta(1)}
. Combining with Eqn. (
C.1
), we have,

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>\epsilon F_{p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{4}(1-n^{-\Theta(1)})+\text{Pr}\left[\lvert\hat{F}_{p}-F_{p}\rvert>\epsilon F_{p}\mid\neg\mathcal{H}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\neg\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{4}(1-n^{-\Theta(1)})+\frac{\delta}{n^{\Theta(1)}}n^{-O(1)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta}{n^{\Theta(1)}},~~~~\text{ since, $\delta=n^{-\omega(1)}$}\kern 5.0pt.

Appendix D
Full Version of Our Lower Bounds

D.1
The
\Omega(n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))
Measurement Lower Bound

We consider the problem of designing a distribution
\mu
over
r\times n
matrices
S
so that for any
fixed vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, from
S\cdot x
, one can estimate
\|x\|_{p}^{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{p}

up to a
(1\pm\epsilon)
factor with probability
at least
1-\delta
. We say the algorithm
(\epsilon,\delta)
-approximates
\|x\|_{p}^{p}
.
We start by showing a lower bound on
r=\Omega(n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))
, assuming that

\log(1/\delta)\leq C\varepsilon^{2}n^{2/p}
, for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
. This assumption is necessary up
to constant factors, as otherwise one can always just take
S
to be the
n\times n
identity matrix.

By Yao’s minimax principle, it
suffices to design two distributions
\alpha
and
\beta
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that

1.

For all
y
in the support of
\alpha
, and all
z
in the support of
\beta
,
\|y\|_{p}^{p}\leq(1-\epsilon)\|z\|_{p}^{p}
.

2.

There is a constant
\kappa>0
so that
for any fixed matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
with
r=\kappa n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta)
,

D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta})<1-\delta
, where
\bar{\alpha}
is the distribution of
Sx
for
x\sim\alpha
and

\bar{\beta}
is the distribution of
Sx
for
x\sim\beta
.

D.1.1
Preliminaries

We use the following lemma (re-stated from Section
2
) concerning distances between multivariate Gaussian distributions.

Lemma 65
(Re-statement of Lemma
D.1.4
.)

Let
P_{1}
denote the
N(0,I_{r})
Gaussian distribution, and
P_{2}
the

N(\tau,I_{r})
Gaussian distribution. Then

D_{TV}(P_{1},P_{2})=\Pr[|N(0,1)|\leq\|\tau\|_{2}/2],

where
N(0,1)
denotes a standard one-dimensional normal random variable.

Proof:

Let
U
be an arbitrary
r\times r
orthonormal matrix.
Note that
D_{TV}(P_{1},P_{2})=D_{TV}(P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime})
, where
P_{i}^{\prime}
is the distribution
of
Ux
, where
x\sim P_{i}
. Let
U
be any such matrix which rotates
\tau
to
\|\tau\|_{2}\cdot e_{1}
,
where
e_{1}
is the first standard unit vector. Then by rotational invariance,

D_{TV}(P_{1},P_{2})=D_{TV}(N(0,1),N(\|\tau\|_{2},1))
. Then by Section 3 of
[
Pol
]
,

D_{TV}(N(0,1),N(\|\tau\|_{2},1)=\Pr[|N(0,1)|\leq\|\tau\|_{2}/2].

We also need a lemma concerning concentration of
\|x\|_{p}
for
x\in N(0,I_{n})
.

Lemma 66

For
x\in N(0,I_{n})
, for all
t\geq 0
we have

\Pr[|\|x\|_{p}-{\bf E}[\|x\|_{p}]|\geq t]\leq 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}.

Proof:

Say that a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
L
-Lipshitz with respect to the Euclidean norm
if
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq L\|x-y\|_{2}
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We invoke the following standard theorem on
L
-Lipshitz functions with respect to the Euclidean norm

Theorem 67

(see, e.g., Theorem 2.4 of
[
Wai
]
)
Let
x\sim N(0,I_{n})
and let
f
be
L
-Lipshitz with respect to the Euclidean norm. Then

\Pr[|f(x)-{\bf E}[f(x)]|\geq t]\leq 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2L^{2}}},

for all
t\geq 0
.

Let
f(x)=\|x\|_{p}
. Then for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\|x\|_{p}-\|y\|_{p}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|x-y\|_{p}\leq\|x-y\|_{2},

where the first inequality is the triangle inequality, and the second uses that
\|z\|_{p}\leq\|z\|_{2}
for
p\geq 2
and
any vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Hence,
f
is
1
-Lipshitz with respect to the Euclidean norm, and applying Theorem
67
,

\Pr[|\|x\|_{p}-{\bf E}[\|x\|_{p}]|\geq t]\leq 2^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}.

Since
{\bf E}_{X\sim N(0,1)}[|X|^{p}]
is a positive constant for any constant
p
, we have that
{\bf E}[\|x\|_{p}^{p}]=\Theta(n)

and
{\bf Var}[\|x\|_{p}^{p}]=O(n)
. Consequently, by Chebyshev’s inequality there is an absolute constant
\kappa>0

so that with probability at least
1/2
, it holds that
\frac{n}{\kappa}\leq\|x\|_{p}^{p}\leq\kappa n
.
Therefore because of the tail bounds given in
Lemma
66
, it follows that

{\bf E}_{x\sim N(0,I_{n})}[\|x\|_{p}]=\Theta(n^{1/p})
. We let
E_{n}={\bf E}_{x\sim N(0,I_{n})}[\|x\|_{p}]
.

D.1.2
The Hard Distribution

Case 1:

Suppose
y=n^{1/p}e_{I}+x
, where
x\sim N(0,I_{n})
and
I
is independent and uniformly random in
[n]=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.
Let
\alpha^{\prime}
be the distribution of
y
.
Then
\|y\|_{p}^{p}=(n^{1/p}e_{I}-x_{I})^{p}+\|\bar{x}\|_{p}^{p}
, where
\bar{x}
denotes the vector
x
with the
I
-th coordinate
removed. Let
\mathcal{E}

be the event that
|x_{I}|\leq\varepsilon n^{1/p}/p
. By independence of
I
and

x
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{I,x}[\mathcal{E}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1/n)\sum_{i}\Pr_{x}[\mathcal{E}\mid I=i]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1/n)\sum_{i}\Pr_{x}[|x_{i}|\leq\epsilon n^{1/p}/p].

By tail bounds for a standard normal random variable,

\Pr[\mathcal{E}]\geq 1-2e^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}n^{2/p}}{2\cdot p^{2}}}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{10},

provided
2e^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}n^{2/p}}{2\cdot p^{2}}}\leq\delta/10
, which holds if
\log(1/\delta)\leq C\varepsilon^{2}n^{2/p}
, for a
sufficiently small constant
C>0
,
as we have assumed. Conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\|y\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(n^{1/p}(1+\epsilon/p))^{p}+\|\bar{x}\|_{p}^{p}\leq n(1+2\epsilon)+\|\bar{x}\|_{p}^{p},

using that
(1+x)^{p}\leq 1+2px
for
px\leq 1/2
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be the event that

\|\bar{x}\|_{p}\leq E_{n-1}+10\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
. By Lemma
66
,
\Pr[\mathcal{F}]\geq 1-\delta/10
. Note also
that
10\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\leq 10\sqrt{C}\varepsilon n^{1/p}
, under our assumption on
\log(1/\delta)
, and for a sufficiently small
constant
C>0
,
this is at most
(\varepsilon/p)E_{n-1}
, using that
E_{n-1}=\Theta(n^{1/p})
. Hence, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\|y\|_{p}^{p}\leq n(1+2\epsilon)+E_{n-1}^{p}(1+\epsilon/p)^{p}=(1+2\epsilon)\cdot(n+E_{n-1}^{p}).

(155)

Case 2:

Now let
z=(1+C^{\prime}\epsilon)n^{1/p}e_{I}+x
, where
x\sim N(0,I_{n})
and
I
is independent and uniformly random in
[n]
.
Here
C^{\prime}>0
be a sufficiently large constant. Let
\beta^{\prime}
be the distribution of
z
.
Then
\|z\|_{p}^{p}=((1+C^{\prime}\epsilon)n^{1/p}e_{I}+x_{I})^{p}+\|\bar{x}\|_{p}^{p}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
be the event that
|x_{I}|\leq\varepsilon n^{1/p}/p
. As for the event
\mathcal{E}
,
we have
\Pr[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}]\geq 1-\delta/10
. Also, let

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be the event hat
\|\bar{x}\|_{p}\geq E_{n-1}-10\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
. As in the previous paragraph, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}]\geq 1-\delta/10

and conditioned on
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, that

\|z\|_{p}^{p}\geq(1+C^{\prime}\epsilon)n(1-\epsilon/p)^{p}+E_{n-1}^{p}(1-\epsilon/p)^{p}\geq(1+C^{\prime}\epsilon/2)n+(1-\epsilon)E_{n-1}^{p},

where here we use Bernoulli’s inequality that
(1+x)^{p}\geq(1+xp)
for
p\geq-1
, and that
C^{\prime}>0
is sufficiently large.
As argued above,
E_{n-1}^{p}=O(n)
, and consequently for large enough
C^{\prime}
, we have that

\displaystyle\|z\|_{p}^{p}\geq(1+4\epsilon)(n+E_{n-1}^{p}).

(156)

D.1.3
Conditioning the Cases:

Let
\alpha
be the distribution of
\alpha^{\prime}
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
. Similarly, let
\beta
be the distribution of

\beta^{\prime}
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. Recall that for a distribution
\gamma
on
x
, the distribution
\bar{\gamma}
is the
distribution of
Sx
.

Combining (
155
) and (
156
) it follows that any algorithm which can
(\epsilon,\delta)
-approximate
\|x\|_{p}^{p}

of an arbitrary vector
x
can also be used to decide, with probability at least
1-\delta
, if
x
is drawn from
\alpha
or if

x
is drawn from
\beta
. Consequently,
D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta})\geq 1-\delta
.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})+D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\alpha})+D_{TV}(\bar{\beta},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})+D_{TV}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})+D_{TV}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})+\delta/10+\delta/10

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})+\delta/5,

where the first inequality is the triangle inequality, the second uses the data processing inequality, and the third uses
that
E\cap F
and
E^{\prime}\cap F^{\prime}
are the events that realize the variation distance in the two cases.
Therefore, to obtain our lower bound, it suffices to show for small sketching dimension
r

that
D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})<1-2\delta
.

D.1.4
Completing the Argument

Let us fix an
r\times n
matrix
S
. Without loss of generality we can assume the rows of
S
are orthonormal, since we can always
perform a change of basis to its rowspace to make the rows orthonormal, and any change of basis preserves
D_{TV}(Sx,Sy)
.

We let
\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i}
denote the distribution of
\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}
conditioned on
I=i
, and similarly define

\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{TV}(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i},\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i})\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i},\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i}).

(157)

To complete the argument, as argued in Section
D.1.2
, we just need to show that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i},\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i})
is at most
1-2\delta
.
Suppose, towards a contradiction, that it were larger than
1-2\delta
. Since it is an average of
n
summands, each bounded by
1
,
it implies that for at least
1-3\delta
fraction of summands, the summand value is at least
1-3\delta
. Indeed, otherwise the summation
would be at most

(1-3\delta)^{2}+3\delta\leq 1-3\delta+9\delta^{2}<1-2\delta
, where the final inequality follows for
\delta
smaller than
a small enough constant. This is a contradiction.

On the other hand, since
\|S\|_{F}^{2}=r
, by averaging, for at least
n/2
columns
S_{i}
of
S
, we have
\|S_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2r/n
. Since
\delta

is smaller than a small enough constant, we can assume
1-3\delta\geq 2/3
, and therefore by a union bound there exists an
i^{*}\in[n]

for which both (1)
\|S_{i^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2r/n
and (2)
D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i^{*}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i^{*}})\geq 1-3\delta
.

Since
S
has orthonormal rows,
\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i^{*}}\sim N(n^{1/p}S_{i^{*}},I_{r})
while
\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i^{*}}\sim N((1+C^{\prime}\epsilon)n^{1/p}S_{i^{*}},I_{r})
.
Shifting both distributions by
n^{1/p}S_{i^{*}}
, it follows that

D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i^{*}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i^{*}})=D_{TV}(N(0,I_{r}),N(C^{\prime}\epsilon n^{1/p}S_{i^{*}},I_{R})).

Applying Lemma
D.1.4
,

\displaystyle D_{TV}(N(0,I_{r}),N(C^{\prime}\epsilon n^{1/p}S_{i^{*}},I_{R})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr[|N(0,1)|\leq C^{\prime}\epsilon n^{1/p}\|S_{i^{*}}\|_{2}/2]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[|N(0,1)|\leq C^{\prime}\epsilon n^{1/p}\sqrt{2r/n}/2].

We can assume
C^{\prime}\epsilon n^{1/p}\sqrt{2r/n}/2\geq 2
, as otherwise this probability is
1-\Omega(1)
, which is a contradiction
to it being at least
1-3\delta
for small enough constant
\delta>0
. A standard bound
[
Due10
]
is then
that for
t\geq 2
,

\Pr[|N(0,1)|>t]\geq\frac{e^{-t^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{2t}.

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[|N(0,1)|\leq C^{\prime}\epsilon n^{1/p}\sqrt{2r/n}/2]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1-2\frac{e^{-(C^{\prime})^{2}\epsilon^{2}n^{2/p}r/(2n)}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{C^{\prime}\epsilon n^{1/p}\sqrt{2r/n}}.

It follows that if
r=o(n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta)
, this probability is strictly less than
1-3\delta
.

Let us recap the argument. We found an
i^{*}
with two properties:
(1)
\|S_{i_{*}}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2r/n
, and
(2)
D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i_{*}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i_{*}})\geq 1-3\delta
.
Using (1), we were able to apply Lemma to upper bound
D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i^{*}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i^{*}})

by a quantity that was strictly less than
1-3\delta
, thereby contradicting (2). It follows that there cannot
exist an
i^{*}
, which means that our hypothesis in (
157
) did not hold. Consequently,

D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}},\bar{\beta^{\prime}})=D_{TV}(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i},\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i})\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}D_{TV}(\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i},\bar{\beta^{\prime}}_{i})\leq 1-2\delta
, and so by the argument in
Section
D.1.3
the proof is complete.

D.2
The
\Omega(n^{1-2/p}\varepsilon^{-2/p}(\log^{2/p}1/\delta)\log n)
Measurement Lower Bound

We can assume
\log(1/\delta)\leq C\varepsilon^{2}n^{2/p}
for a small enough constant
C>0
, as otherwise
the lower bound we proved in Section
D.1
is
\Omega(n)
, and one can always let
S
be the
n\times n
identity matrix
which would be optimal up to a constant factor in this regime. We refer to this as
\delta
-
Bound1
. Note also that since
p>2
,
this implies
n=\omega(\log(1/\delta))
, which implies
E_{n-t}=\Theta(n^{1/p})
whenever
t=O(\log(1/\delta))
. We use this
fact later.

Furthermore, we can give two other bounds on
\delta
, similar to
\delta
-
Bound1
.

First, we can assume that
n^{1-2/p}\varepsilon^{-2/p}(\log^{2/p}1/\delta)\log n=\Omega(n^{1-2/p}\epsilon^{-2}\log 1/\delta)
,
as otherwise the lower bound in Section
D.1
is stronger.
This is equivalent to assuming
\log(1/\delta)\leq C\epsilon^{2-2/p}(\log^{2/p}1/\delta)\log n
for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
.
We refer to this as
\delta
-
Bound2
.

Second, we can assume
n^{1-2/p}\varepsilon^{-2/p}(\log^{2/p}1/\delta)\log n\leq Cn
for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
, as otherwise the lower bound
we are proving is
\Omega(n)
and one can always just let
S
be the
n\times n
identity matrix which would be optimal up to a constant
factor in this regime. This assumption is equivalent to
\log^{2/p}1/\delta\leq C\varepsilon^{2/p}n^{2/p}/\log n
. We refer to this as
\delta
-
Bound3
.

In fact, we will need to assume
\delta
-
Bound4
, which is that
\log(1/\delta)\leq(n^{1-2/p}\varepsilon^{-2/p}(\log^{2/p}1/\delta)\log n)^{1/4}n^{-c^{\prime}}
,
for a sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime}>0
. Since
p>2
is an absolute constant, independent of
n
, this just states that
\delta\geq 2^{-n^{c^{\prime\prime}}}
for a sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime\prime}>0
.
We note that
\delta
-
Bound4
implies some of the bounds above, but we state it separately since unlike the previous three bounds on
\delta
, which
are optimal, it may be possible to relax this one for a larger constant
c^{\prime\prime}>0
.

D.2.1
Preliminaries

Let
p
and
q
be probability density functions of continuous distributions. The
\chi^{2}
-divergence from
p
to
q
is

\chi^{2}(p,q)=\int_{x}\left(\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}-1\right)^{2}q(x)dx.

Fact 68

(
[
Tsy08
]
, p.90)
For any two distributions
p
and
q
, we have
D_{TV}(p,q)\leq\sqrt{\chi^{2}(p,q)}
.

We need a fact about the distance between a Gaussian location mixture to a Gaussian distribution.

Fact 69

(p.97 of
[
IS03
]
)
Let
p
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then

\chi^{2}(N(0,I_{n})\ast p,N(0,I_{n}))={\bf E}[e^{\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle}]-1,

where
X
and
X^{\prime}
are independently drawn from
p
.

D.2.2
The Hard Distribution

Let
T
be a sample of
t\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\log_{3}(1/\sqrt{\delta})
coordinates
i\in[n]
without replacement.

Case 1:

Suppose
y\sim N(0,I_{n})
, and let
\alpha^{\prime}
be the distribution of
y
.
Let
\bar{y}
denote the vector
y
with the coordinates in the set
T
removed, and let
x
be
y
restricted to the coordinates in
T
.
By the triangle inequality,
\|y\|_{p}\leq\|\bar{y}\|_{p}+\|x\|_{p}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that
\|\bar{y}\|_{p}\leq E_{n-t}+10\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
.
By Lemma
66
,
\Pr[\mathcal{E}]\geq 1-\delta/10
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be the event that
\|x\|_{p}\leq E_{t}+10\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
.
Again by Lemma
66
,
\Pr[\mathcal{F}]\geq 1-\delta/10
.
Note also that
E_{t}=\Theta(t^{1/p})\leq\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
using the definition of
t
and that
p>2
.
Consequently, if
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
occur, then

\|y\|_{p}\leq E_{n-t}+21\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}.

By
\delta
-
Bound1
, we have

\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\leq C^{1/2}\varepsilon n^{1/p}
for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
. This implies that

21\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\leq(\epsilon/p)E_{n-t}
, using that
E_{n-t}=\Theta(n^{1/p})
.
Consequently, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
, we have that

\displaystyle\|y\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon/p)^{p}E_{n-t}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+2\epsilon)E_{n-t}^{p},

where the second inequality follows for
\epsilon
less than a sufficiently small positive constant.

Case 2:

Let
z=x+\sum_{i\in T}\frac{C^{\prime}\epsilon^{1/p}E_{n-t}}{t^{1/p}}e_{i},
where
x\sim N(0,I_{n})
.
Note that
x
and
T
are independent. Also,
C^{\prime}>0
is a sufficiently large constant.
Let
\beta^{\prime}
be the distribution of
z
. Let
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
be the event
that for all
i\in T
,
|x_{i}|\leq\epsilon^{1/p}n^{1/p}/(pt^{1/p})
. Then

\Pr[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}]\geq 1-t\cdot 2e^{-\frac{\epsilon^{2/p}n^{2/p}}{2p^{2}t^{2/p}}}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{10},

which holds provided
\log(1/\delta)\leq C\frac{\epsilon^{2/p}n^{2/p}}{\log^{2/p}1/\delta}
for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
. To see that
the latter holds, note that it is equivalent to the constraint that

\log^{1-2/p}(1/\delta)\leq C\epsilon^{2/p}n^{2/p}
